approach, this manuscript focuses on preserving a precise asymptotic constant. All results generalize the van Trees inequality, and imply the well-known semiparametric efficiency bound (Proposition
1
). Theorem
7
also implies local minimax results for irregular models (Lemma
10
).

The flexibility of the proposed lower bounds offers many potential applications, especially for non-differentiable functionals or estimation under irregularity. For example, one may consider the minimax lower bound for non-pathwise differentiable functionals or the irregularity that arises on the boundary of projection operations. See a short remark in Section
K
of the Supplementary Material. A similar local asymptotic minimax lower bound was recently derived in the context of plug-in estimators
(
Fang,, 2014
)
, but there remain many open problems for general estimators. Finally, as none of Theorem
5
–
7
requires the IID observation, the potential application to the sharp minimax lower bound under non-IID observations would also be of interest.

As we discussed in Section
1.2
, a precise minimax lower bound for non-differentiable functional estimation was also discovered by
Cai and Low, (2011)
, who studied the mixture extension of the constrained risk inequality
(
Brown and Low,, 1996
)
.
Their method is also attractive as two mixture distributions are constructed based on moment-matching priors, enabling various analytic tools from approximation theory
(
Wu and Yang,, 2020
)
. The current result only considers a single prior over a parametric path, and it would be interesting to explore a technique based on moment-matching priors.

Finally,
Levit, (2010)
discusses the importance of non-asymptotic constants in the minimax paradigm. In particular,
Levit, (2010)
observes that different lower bounding methods provide sharp constants according to small, moderate, or large sample sizes. Although the results in this manuscript are valid non-asymptotically and converge to the sharp constant in the limit, we did not study the sharpness of the derived constants for different sample sizes. While
Levit, (2010)
focuses on bounded Gaussian mean estimations, the proposed lower bounds can be extended to non-asymptotic analysis for more complex functional estimation problems, which is an important direction for future work.

Appendix A
Proof of the lower bound based on the approximation

We briefly review the setting and notation to which we frequently refer. Suppose we observe a random variable
X
from an unknown distribution
P_{\theta}
, which belongs to a statistical model
\mathcal{P}:=\{P_{t}:t\in\Theta\}
defined on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
. Here,
\mathcal{A}
denotes
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\psi:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k}
denote the vector-valued function of interest. In other words, the target estimand is described as the evaluation of the functional at the population parameter
\psi(P_{\theta})
.

A.1
Proof of Lemma
4

Let
(\Theta,\mathcal{T})
be a measurable space with respect to the base measure
\nu
where
\mathcal{T}
is a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Theta
. We denote by
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}(\Theta)
be a collection of probability measures on
\Theta
equipped with a density function with respect to the base measure on
\Theta
. We define
\Phi:=\{\phi:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k}\}
to be a collection of arbitrary functionals that maps from
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\phi\in\Phi
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left(\int\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\left(\int\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\phi(P_{\theta})+\phi(P_{\theta})-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\left(\int\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\phi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}-\left(\int\|\phi(P_{\theta})-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}

by the reverse triangle inequality. When the lower bound is negative, we replace it with the trivial lower bound of zero.
As the choice of
\phi
was arbitrary, we conclude by taking the supremum over
\phi
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\sup_{\phi\in\Phi,\,Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\,\left[\left(\int\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left\|T(X)-\phi(P_{\theta})\right\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}-\left(\int\|\psi(\theta)-\phi(\theta)\|^{2}dQ(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\right]_{+}^{2}.

A.2
Proof of Theorem
5

Assume that a collection of probability measures
\mathcal{Q}^{\dagger}
supported on
\Theta_{0}
satisfies Definition
1
. Note that
\mathcal{Q}^{\dagger}
depends on
\phi
.
For any absolutely continuous function
\phi
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\dagger}
, we evoke the multivariate van Trees inequality (Theorem 12 of
Gassiat and Stoltz, (2024)
) under the Hellinger differentiability of the statistical model
P_{t}
for all
t\in\Theta_{0}
. For any vector norm
\|\cdot\|
, let
\|\cdot\|_{*}
be its dual norm. Then, for any vector
u
such that
\|u\|_{*}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\int\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left\|T(X)-\phi(\theta)\right\|^{2}\,dQ

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Theta_{0}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|u^{\top}\big(T(X)-\phi(P_{\theta})\big)|^{2}\,dQ

(17)

\displaystyle\geq u^{\top}\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\phi(t)\,dQ\right)^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+\int_{\Theta_{0}}\mathcal{I}(t)\,dQ\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\phi(t)\,dQ\right)u

(18)

by the van Trees inequality. Combining this result with Lemma
4
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left[\left(u^{\top}\Gamma_{Q,\phi}u\right)^{1/2}-\left(\int\|\phi(P_{\theta})-\psi(P_{\theta})\|^{2}\,dQ\right)^{1/2}\right]_{+}^{2}

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{Q,\phi}:=\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\phi(t)\,dQ\right)^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+\int_{\Theta_{0}}\mathcal{I}(t)\,dQ\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\phi(t)\,dQ\right).

(19)

This holds for any
\phi\in\Phi_{\textrm{ac}}
,
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\dagger}
and
u
such that
\|u\|_{*}\leq 1
. We thus conclude the claim by taking supremum over them.

Appendix B
Proofs of the mixture extensions of the HCR bound

Let
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the vector-valued function of interest, and let
Q
be a prior distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
equipped with a density function. We define two probability measures on a product space
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

d\mathbb{P}_{0}(x,\theta):=dP_{\theta}(x)\,dQ(\theta)\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994ptd\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,\theta):=dP_{\theta+h}(x)\,dQ(\theta+h).

B.1
Proof of Theorem
6

For each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we define a mixture distribution as
\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0}+\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}
with the corresponding density function given by

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,\theta):=(1-\lambda)d\mathbb{P}_{0}(x,\theta)+\lambda d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,\theta).

Throughout the proof, we denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
the expectation under
P_{\theta}
with a fixed parameter
\theta
, by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}
the expectation under the joint probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{h}
, and by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}
the expectation under the joint mixture probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}
.
For any measurable functions
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
T:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,dP_{t+h}(x)\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}-h)}\left(T(x)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dP_{u}(x)\,dQ(u)

where
\mathbb{R}^{d}-h
is the set
\{u-h:u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. In particular, we have
\mathbb{R}^{d}-h=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We then obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}T(X)\,dQ(t)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u-h)\,dQ(u).

Similarly under the mixture distribution
\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,d\mathbb{P}_{0}(x,t)+\lambda\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(T(x)-\psi(t)\right)\,dP_{t}(x)\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\lambda\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}-h)}\left(T(x)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dP_{u}(x)\,dQ(u)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}T(X)\,dQ(t)+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}T(X)\,dQ(t)-(1-\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u)\,dQ(u)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u-h)\,dQ(u)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}T(X)\,dQ(t)-(1-\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u)\,dQ(u)-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u-h)\,dQ(u).

Therefore, it follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u)\,dQ(u)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(u-h)\,dQ(u)\right).

(20)

Next, we consider the following ratio of the joint probability over
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

\displaystyle D_{h,\lambda}:(x,t)\mapsto\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,t)-d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)}{d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)}.

It follows from (
20
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D_{h,\lambda}(X,\theta)\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u).

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the dual norm of the above display, we obtain

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)^{2}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right)\,dQ(t)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D_{h,\lambda}(X,\theta)\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{a:\|a\|\leq 1}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D_{h,\lambda}(X,\theta)a^{\top}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D_{h,\lambda}^{2}(X,\theta)\right\}\sup_{a:\|a\|\leq 1}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}|a^{\top}(T(X)-\psi(\theta))|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D_{h,\lambda}^{2}(X,\theta)\right\}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}\right\}.

(21)

We now analyze each term in the last expression. First, by the definition of the chi-squared divergence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}D^{2}(X,\theta)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,t)-d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)}{d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)}\right)^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)=\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0}).

Next, for the second term of the upper bound in (
21
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|T(x)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\lambda}(x,t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|T(x)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dP_{t}(x)\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\lambda\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|T(x)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dP_{t+h}(x)\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|T(x)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dP_{t}(x)\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\lambda\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|T(x)-\psi(t)-\psi(t+h)+\psi(t+h)\right\|^{2}\,dP_{t+h}(x)\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq(1-\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\lambda\bigg((1+L)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}-h}\mathbb{E}_{u}\|T(X)-\psi(u)\|^{2}\,dQ(u)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(1+1/L)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}-h}\|\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(u)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1+L\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)+\lambda(1+1/L)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)

where we use
(a+b)^{2}\leq(1+L)a^{2}+(1+1/L)b^{2}
for any
L\geq 0
, which follows from
2ab\leq a^{2}L+b^{2}/L
. Putting all intermediate results together, the inequality (
21
) implies

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:vt_cauchy_schwarz}\implies

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right)\,dQ(t)\right\|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0})}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq(1+L\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)+\lambda(1+1/L)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\frac{1}{1+L\lambda}\bigg[\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right)\,dQ(t)\right\|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0})}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt-\lambda(1+1/L)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)\bigg]_{+}.

Since the Bayes risk is bounded by minimax risk for any prior distribution
Q
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left[\frac{\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right)\,dQ(t)\right\|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0})}-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right]_{+}.

As the introduced parameter
L\geq 0
is arbitrary, we can optimize its choice. First, we observe that the lower bound is in the form

\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left[A-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}B\right]_{+}.

where

\displaystyle A:=\frac{\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right)\,dQ(t)\right\|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\lambda\mathbb{P}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{P}_{0})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994ptB:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t).

When
\lambda=0
, the above display takes
A^{2}
regardless of the value of
L
. The argument for
B=0
is also similar. Hence we focus on the case with
\lambda,B>0
. First we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left[A-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}B\right]_{+}=\frac{\lambda^{2}B}{1+L\lambda}\left[\left(\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}A}{\lambda^{2}B}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{L\lambda}\right]_{+}=\frac{\lambda^{2}B}{1+L\lambda}\left[\Gamma-\frac{1}{L\lambda}\right]_{+}

where

\Gamma:=\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}A}{\lambda^{2}B}-\frac{1}{\lambda}.

The optimization of
L\geq 0
is thus equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\geq 0}\,\left\{\frac{1}{1+\ell}\left(\Gamma-\frac{1}{\ell}\right)\right\}.

We later check if the attained maximum is positive otherwise replace the optima with zero. The optimal value of
\ell
is given by

\displaystyle\ell^{*}=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+\Gamma}}{\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{when}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma>0.

The corresponding optima is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\ell^{*}}\left(\Gamma-\frac{1}{\ell^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma}{\Gamma+1+\sqrt{1+\Gamma}}\left(\Gamma-\frac{\Gamma}{1+\sqrt{1+\Gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma^{2}\sqrt{1+\Gamma}}{\sqrt{1+\Gamma}\left(\sqrt{\Gamma+1}+1\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sqrt{\Gamma+1}-1\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{\Gamma+1}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(\sqrt{\Gamma+1}+1\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{\Gamma+1}-1\right)^{2}.

This is non-negative when
\Gamma>0
and thus it is a valid optimum for
(1+\ell)^{-1}[\Gamma-1/\ell]_{+}
. Plugging this result into the original expression, we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}B\left(\sqrt{\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}A}{\lambda^{2}B}-\frac{1}{\lambda}+1}-1\right)^{2}=\left(\sqrt{(1-\lambda)\left\{A-\lambda(A+B)\right\}}-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}B}\right)^{2}.

When
\Gamma\leq 0
, or equivalently
(1-\lambda)^{2}A\leq\lambda B
, the lower bound becomes zero. As
T
and
h
only appear on one side of the inequality, we conclude the claim by taking the infimum over
T
and the supremum over
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
7

Following the analogous derivation leading up to equation (
20
) given by the proof of Theorem
6
with the special case of
\lambda=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u).

(22)

Next, we consider the density ratio of the joint probability over
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by

\displaystyle D_{h}:=(x,t)\mapsto\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(x,t)-d\mathbb{P}_{0}(x,t)}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}(x,t)}.

By the application of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to (
22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}D_{h}(X,\theta)\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(X,\theta)-d\mathbb{P}_{0}(X,\theta)}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}(X,\theta)}\right)\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(X,\theta)}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}(X,\theta)}}-1\right)\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(X,\theta)}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}(X,\theta)}}+1\right)\left(T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{h}(X,\theta)}{d\mathbb{P}_{0}(X,\theta)}}+1\right)^{2}\left\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right\|^{2}\right\}H^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}\right)H^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})

(23)

where we apply the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
in the last step. We now analyze two terms inside the parenthesis of the above display. Since minimax risk gives an upper bound of Bayes risk, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|T(X)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}.

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{h}}\left\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t)-\psi(t+h)+\psi(t+h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t+h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t+h)-\psi(t)\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+2\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t+h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}\,\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t+h)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\left(\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\left\|T(X)-\psi(t+h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}+\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t+h)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}\right)^{2}

where the inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore (
23
) implies the following:

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:vt_cauchy_schwarz_hellinger}\implies\frac{\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u)\right\|^{2}}{2H^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\left(\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{t+h}\|T(X)-\psi(t+h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}+\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t+h)-\psi(t)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t+h)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\left(\sqrt{\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}}+\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}-h}\left\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}}+\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t)}\right)^{2}

where the last step used the fact that
\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t)}\geq 0
.
We thus obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u)\right\|^{2}}{2H^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}}+\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\|^{2}\,dQ(t)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\left[\frac{\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\psi(u)-\psi(u-h)\right)\,dQ(u)\right\|}{2H(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\psi(t)-\psi(t-h)\right\|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}_{+}.

As with the proof of Theorem
6
, the function
T
and
h
only appear on one side of the inequality, and we conclude the claim by taking the infimum over
T
and the supremum over
h\in\Theta
.

B.3
Proof of Lemma
8

The main idea is provided by Theorem 6.1 of
Ibragimov and Has’minskii, (1981)
. Here, we derive the improved constant. We focus on the estimation of the real-valued functional
\psi:\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
for
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. An analogous proof can be applied to each specific choice of the vector norm
\|\cdot\|
(See remark 6.1 of
Ibragimov and Has’minskii, (1981)
).
For any real-valued measurable function
T:=\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, two points in the parameter space
\{\theta,\theta+h\}\in\Theta
, and an arbitrary scalar constant
C
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathcal{X}}(T(x)-C)\,\left(dP_{\theta+h}(x)-dP_{\theta}(x)\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathcal{X}}(T(x)-C)\left(dP^{1/2}_{\theta+h}(x)-dP^{1/2}_{\theta}(x)\right)\left(dP^{1/2}_{\theta+h}(x)+dP^{1/2}_{\theta}(x)\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-C|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)+\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-C|^{2}\,dP_{\theta}(x)\right).

(24)

where the last inequalities follow by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
. The first integral on the right-hand side can be written out as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left|T(x)-C\right|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)+\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-C|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)+|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-C|^{2}.

We also have the following standard bias-variance decomposition:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}-|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}.

Putting together, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left|T(x)-C\right|^{2}\,dP_{\theta+h}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}-|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|+|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-C|^{2}.

By repeating the analogous argument for
\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T(x)-C|^{2}\,dP_{\theta}(x)
and plugging them into (
24
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leq 2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})\left(\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}-|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-C|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}-|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}+|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)-C|^{2}\left.\right).

Since the above inequality holds for an arbitrary scalar constant
C
, we choose
C
to minimize the upper bound. As the optimal
C^{*}
is attained by
C^{*}=1/2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)+\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)\right)
, we can further simplify the expression as

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})\left(\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\left.-|d(\theta+h)|^{2}-|d(\theta)|^{2}+\frac{\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)\right|^{2}}{2}\right)\,.

(25)

where
d(t):=\mathbb{E}_{t}T(X)-\psi(t)
.
Hence, the above display immediately implies the following:

(
25
)

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X)\right|^{2}+|d(\theta+h)|^{2}+|d(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}\left|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)+d(\theta+h)-d(\theta)\right|^{2}+|d(\theta+h)|^{2}+|d(\theta)|^{2}.

The existing proofs by
Ibragimov and Has’minskii, (1981)
and
Lin et al., (2019)
proceed by splitting the analysis into two cases: (i)
\max\{|d(\theta+h)|,|d(\theta)|\}<1/4|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)|
and (ii)
|d(\theta)|>1/4|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)|
where the leading constant
1/4
for the boundary was chosen for convenience by
Ibragimov and Has’minskii, (1981)
as they did not focus on the optimal constant. We instead optimize this boundary to obtain a sharper constant.

We now define the following function:

\eta(x,y):=A|B+x-y|^{2}+|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}

with
A:=(1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta}))/(2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta}))
and
B:=\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)
. The lower bound can be written as

E_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+E_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}\geq\eta(d(\theta+h),d(\theta)).

This further implies that

E_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+E_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}\geq\min_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\,\eta(x,y).

The minimizer of
\eta
is given by
x^{*}=-y^{*}=-\frac{2AB}{4A+2}
and hence we have,

\min_{x,y}\eta(x,y)=\frac{4AB^{2}}{(4A+2)^{2}}+\frac{8A^{2}B^{2}}{(4A+2)^{2}}=\frac{4AB^{2}(1+2a)}{(4A+2)^{2}}=\frac{AB^{2}}{2A+1}.

Additionally, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{xx}\eta=2A+2,\hskip 9.24994pt\partial_{xy}\eta=-2A,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\partial_{yy}\eta=2A+2

and the discriminant is given by
(2A+2)^{2}-4A^{2}=4(2A+1)
. Therefore, the optima is at a saddle point when
A<-1/2
. The constant
A
is defined as
(1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta}))/(2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta}))
, which is monotone decreasing with respect to
H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})
and attains the minimum
-1/4
at
H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})=2
. Thus the minimizer of
\eta
is well-defined for all values of
A
and thereby all values of
H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})
. Putting it together, the minimum of the function
\eta
with respect to
d(\theta+h)
and
d(\theta)
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{AB^{2}}{2A+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{2H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}\left|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)\right|^{2}\left(\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}+1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{2}\left|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)\right|^{2}.

When
H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})>1
, the leading constant becomes negative and we can replace it with a trivial lower bound of zero.
Therefore, we conclude

\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}\geq\left[\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{2}\right]_{+}|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}.

Since minimax risk gives an upper bound of the average of risks at arbitrary two points, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\Theta}\,\mathbb{E}_{t}|T(X)-\psi(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\{\theta,\theta+h\}\in\Theta}\,\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\theta+h}|T(X)-\psi(\theta+h)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\{\theta,\theta+h\}\in\Theta}\,\left[\frac{1-H^{2}(P_{\theta+h},P_{\theta})}{4}\right]_{+}|\psi(\theta+h)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}.

As
T
and
h
only appear on one side of the inequality, we conclude the claim by taking the infimum over
T
and the supremum over any pair of parameters
\theta,\theta+h\in\Theta
.
An analogous proof can be extended to a vector-valued functional with a general vector norm
\|\cdot\|
. We only provide a result with a real-valued functional since the optimal constant depends on the choice of the norm as it requires the derivative with respect to the norm.

Appendix C
Additional technical facts about divergence metrics

This manuscript primarily focuses on two divergence metrics, namely the chi-squared divergence and the Hellinger distance. This section discusses sufficient conditions that imply certain limiting behaviors of these metrics over a parametric path as it passes through
P_{0}
. The base measure for the sample space
\mathcal{X}
is denoted by
\nu
.

C.1
Regularity conditions

We first extend the standard notion of absolute continuity of a univariate function to a multivariate function as follows:

Definition 2

(Multivariate absolute continuity)
.

A function
\omega:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is absolutely continuous over an open
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-ball
B(\theta,\delta)
if for all
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the induced univariate function
t\mapsto\omega(\theta+tu)
is absolutely continuous over
0<t<\delta
.

The concept of multivariate absolute continuity has been explored in various real analysis literature. For instance,
Malỳ, (1999)
and
Hencl, (2004)
extend the classical
\delta
-
\varepsilon
definition of absolute continuity by considering the oscillation of the functions over
d
-dimensional balls. Additionally,
Šremr, (2010)
presents a similar extension using
d
-dimensional hyper-cubes. We do not claim that Definition
2
is the most general definition of multivariate absolutely continuous functions.

We introduce the following regularity conditions:

(A2)

There exists
\delta>0
such that

(a)

for
\nu
-almost everywhere, the mapping
t\mapsto dP_{t}
is absolutely continuous over an open
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-ball
B([0],\delta)
with the gradient
t\mapsto u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}
for each
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

(b)

for all
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the gradient is continuous in
t
such that
\lim_{\|t\|\longrightarrow 0}u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}=u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{0}
for
\nu
-almost everywhere, and

(c)

for all
0<t<\delta
and
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
dP_{0}(x)=0
implies
u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}(x)=0
, and

\displaystyle\int\sup_{0<t_{1},t_{2}<\delta}u^{\top}\left(\frac{\dot{\rho}_{t_{1},u}\dot{\rho}_{t_{2},u}^{\top}}{dP_{0}}\right)u\,d\nu<\infty.

(A3)

There exits
\delta>0
such that

(a)

for
\nu
-almost everywhere, the mapping
t\mapsto dP^{1/2}_{t}
is absolutely continuous over an open
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-ball
B([0],\delta)
with the gradient
t\mapsto u^{\top}\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}
for each
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

(b)

for all
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the gradient is continuous such that
\lim_{\|t\|\longrightarrow 0}u^{\top}\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}=u^{\top}\dot{\gamma}_{0}
for
\nu
-almost everywhere, and

\displaystyle\int\sup_{0<t_{1},t_{2}<\delta}u^{\top}\dot{\gamma}_{t_{1},u}\dot{\gamma}_{t_{2},u}^{\top}u\,d\nu<\infty.

(A4)

The statistical model
\{P_{t}:t\in\Theta\}
is Hellinger differentiable.

We claim that

\ref{as:regular_density_iso}\implies\ref{as:regular_root_density_iso}\implies\ref{as:hellinder-diff},

meaning that
(A2)
is most restrictive and
(A4)
is most general.

Proof of
(A2)

\implies

(A3)
.

Given a small positive scalar
\eta>0
, we define
dP_{t,\eta}:=dP_{t}+\eta
. The resulting object is no longer a probability density since it does not integrate to one. Since
dP_{t,\eta}
is bounded away from zero, the gradient of
dP^{1/2}_{t,\eta}
exists and is given by
\frac{1}{2}\left\{\dot{\rho}_{t,u}/(dP_{t}+\eta)^{1/2}\right\}
.
It now follows for any
0<b<\delta
that

\displaystyle dP^{1/2}_{t,\eta}(ub)-dP^{1/2}_{t,\eta}(0)=\int_{0}^{b}\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2(dP_{t}+\eta)^{1/2}}\,dt=\int_{0}^{b}\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2(dP_{t}+\eta)^{1/2}}I(dP_{t}>0)\,dt.

The second equality follows by
(A2)
(c) as
dP_{0}(x)=0
implies
u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}(x)=0
, which justifies to insert the indicator
I(dP_{t}>0)
inside the integral. Assuming for a moment that we can invoke the dominated convergence theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\longrightarrow 0}\left\{dP^{1/2}_{t,\eta}(ub)-dP^{1/2}_{t,\eta}(0)\right\}=\int_{0}^{b}\lim_{\eta\longrightarrow 0}\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2(dP_{t}+\eta)^{1/2}}I(dP_{t}>0)\,dt=\int_{0}^{b}\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2dP^{1/2}_{t}}I(dP_{t}>0)\,dt.

Hence, we conclude
(A3)
(a) with

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}=\frac{\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2dP^{1/2}_{t}}I(dP_{t}>0).

(26)

It thus remains to check the condition for the dominated convergence theorem. This follows since we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{b}\left|\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2(dP_{t}+\eta)^{1/2}}I(dP_{t}>0)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{b}\left|\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}}{2(dP_{t})^{1/2}}I(dP_{t}>0)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{b}\frac{u^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}\dot{\rho}_{t,u}^{\top}u}{dP_{t}}I(dP_{t}>0)\,dt\right)^{1/2}.

The last term is integrable by
(A2)
(c) and the dominated convergence theorem holds. The second statement
(A3)
(b) follows directly from (
26
),
(A2)
(b) and
(A2)
(c).
∎

Proof of
(A3)

\implies

(A4)
.

By the absolute continuity of the square root density, it follows

\displaystyle dP_{t}^{1/2}-dP_{0}^{1/2}=\int_{0}^{t}\dot{\gamma}_{s,\mathrm{sign}(t)}\,ds=\int_{0}^{1}\dot{\gamma}_{t\widetilde{s},\mathrm{sign}(t)}t\,d\widetilde{s}

and hence we have

\displaystyle\int\left(dP_{t}^{1/2}-dP_{0}^{1/2}\right)^{2}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=\int\left(\int_{0}^{1}\dot{\gamma}_{t\widetilde{s},\mathrm{sign}(t)}t\,d\widetilde{s}\right)^{2}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=t^{\top}\left\{\int\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\dot{\gamma}_{t\widetilde{s}_{1},\mathrm{sign}(t)}\dot{\gamma}_{t\widetilde{s}_{2},\mathrm{sign}(t)}^{\top}\,d\widetilde{s}_{1}\,d\widetilde{s}_{2}\right)\,d\nu\right\}\,t.

By the assumption that
\lim_{s\longrightarrow 0}\dot{\gamma}_{s,u}=\dot{\gamma}_{0}
for
\nu
-almost where and uniform integrability
s\mapsto u^{\top}\dot{\gamma}_{s,u}
, we conclude

\displaystyle\int\left(dP_{t}^{1/2}-dP_{0}^{1/2}\right)^{2}\,d\nu=t^{\top}\left(\int\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}\,d\nu\right)t+o(\|t\|^{2})

as
\|t\|\longrightarrow 0
. This concludes the claim that the local path
t\mapsto P_{t}
is Hellinger differentiable at
t=0
with
\dot{\xi}_{0}=\dot{\gamma}_{0}
,
\nu
-almost everywhere. As a result, we have

\mathcal{I}(0)=4\int\dot{\xi}_{0}\dot{\xi}_{0}^{\top}\,d\nu=4\int\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}\,d\nu.

∎

Additionally, the converse is not true. For instance, location families with compact support fail to satisfy
(A2)
(c), while they may satisfy
(A3)
under certain conditions. See Example 7.1 of
Polyanskiy and Wu, (2022)
for more details. There is also an example where
(A4)
holds but
(A3)
fails. See Remark
5
in the following subsection.

C.2
Supporting lemmas on divergence metrics

In this section, we provide the supporting lemmas related to the local behaviors of the chi-squared divergence and the Hellinger distance. We provide the derivation for completeness and to unify terminology between
Pollard, (2023)
and
Polyanskiy and Wu, (2022)
. Throughout this section, we use the following notation consistently. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\Theta
be a continuously differentiable mapping and
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}<C
for some universal constant. Unless specified otherwise, we denote the mixture probability measures by

\displaystyle d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}(x,t):=dP^{n}_{\varphi(t)}(x)\,dQ(t)\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{and}\hskip 9.24994ptd\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}(x,t):=dP^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}(x)\,dQ(t+h),

where
P_{\varphi(t)}^{n}
is an
n
-fold product measure of
P_{\varphi(t)}
,
Q(\cdot)
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a bounded and absolutely continuous density function
q
with respect to the base measure on
\Theta
.

Under the regularity conditions from the previous subsection, the following local expansion of divergence metrics can be obtained:

Lemma 15
.

Assume that the paths
h\mapsto P_{\varphi(t+h)}
satisfies
(A2)
and
\nabla\varphi
is continuous at
t
,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}\left(P_{\varphi(t+h)},P_{\varphi(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=h^{\top}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\nabla\varphi(t)h^{\top}+o(\|h\|^{2}).

Lemma 16
.

Assume that the paths
h\mapsto P_{\varphi(t+h)}
satisfies
(A3)
and
\nabla\varphi
is continuous at
t
,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)^{2}h^{\top}\left\{\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\left(\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))+\frac{1-4\lambda}{4\lambda}\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(\varphi(t))\right)\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right\}h+o(\|h\|^{2}).

where
\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(0):=4\int\dot{\gamma}_{0}\,\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}I(dP_{0}=0)\,d\nu
is known as the Fisher defect.

Lemma 17
.

Assume that the paths
h\mapsto dP_{\varphi(t+h)}
satisfies
(A4)
and
\nabla\varphi
is continuous at
t
,

\displaystyle H^{2}\left(P_{\varphi(t+h)},P_{\varphi(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\nabla\varphi(t)h^{\top}+o(\|h\|^{2}).

The main takeaway of the three lemmas is as follows: The local behavior of the Hellinger distance can be established under weaker conditions, while an analogous result under the chi-squared divergence requires more unpleasant regularity conditions. This is one of many reasons why the asymptotic theory according to Hàjek and Le Cam promotes the square roots of density functions as the primary object to investigate.

Remark 5

(On the Fisher defect
\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(\cdot)
)
.

If the parameter
t=0
is an interior point of the parameter space
\Theta
, then
\dot{\gamma}_{0}=0
for
\nu
-almost all
x
in
\{x:dP_{0}(x)=0\}
. Consequently, the Fisher defect must be zero. However, even for a Hellinger differentiable statistical model, it is still possible for the Fisher defect to be non-zero on the boundary. An illustrative example is the Bernoulli distribution with parameter
p^{2}
at
p=0
. Example 7.2 of
Polyanskiy and Wu, (2022)
provides a formal derivation, and Example 18 of
Pollard, (2023)
offers an additional example.

Proof of Lemma
15
.

We define
\mathrm{sign}(x):=x/\|x\|
and
u:=\mathrm{sign}(\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t))
. Since it is assumed that the path
h\mapsto P_{\varphi(t+h)}
is absolutely continuous, there exists a gradient such that

\displaystyle dP_{\varphi(t+h)}-dP_{\varphi(t)}=\int_{\varphi(t)}^{\varphi(t+h)}\dot{\rho}_{s,u}\,ds

for any
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Using this result, the chi-squared divergence can be written out as follows:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{\varphi(t+h)}\|P_{\varphi(t)})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\big(dP_{\varphi(t+h)}-dP_{\varphi(t)}\big)^{2}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\big(\int_{\varphi(t)}^{\varphi(t+h)}\dot{\rho}_{s,u}\,ds\big)^{2}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\left\{\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla\varphi(t+h\widetilde{s})h)^{\top}\dot{\rho}_{\varphi(t+h\widetilde{s}),u}\,d\widetilde{s}\right\}^{2}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}(x)}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=h^{\top}\left(\int\frac{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\varphi^{\top}(t+h\widetilde{s}_{1})\dot{\rho}_{\varphi(t+h\widetilde{s}_{1}),u}\dot{\rho}^{\top}_{\varphi(t+h\widetilde{s}_{2}),u}\nabla\varphi(t+h\widetilde{s}_{2})\,d\widetilde{s}_{1}\,d\widetilde{s}_{2}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}}\,d\nu\right)h.

The last steps use the change of variables. By the uniform integrability assumption and the continuity of
\dot{\rho}_{t}
, both asserted by
(A2)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{\varphi(t+h)}\|P_{\varphi(t)})

\displaystyle=h^{\top}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\left(\int\frac{\dot{\rho}_{\varphi(t)}\dot{\rho}^{\top}_{\varphi(t)}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}}\,d\nu\right)\nabla\varphi(t)h+o(\|h\|^{2})

by the dominated convergence. As the middle term in the parenthesis is finite under
(A2)
, Lemma 6 of
Gassiat and Stoltz, (2024)
implies that the path
dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t)}
is Hellinger differentiable with the gradient

\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\dot{\rho}_{\varphi(t)}}{dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t)}}I(dP_{\varphi(t)}>0).

We can use this to show that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))=4\int\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}^{\top}\,d\nu=\left(\int\frac{\dot{\rho}_{\varphi(t)}\dot{\rho}^{\top}_{\varphi(t)}}{dP_{\varphi(t)}}\,d\nu\right)

and this leads us to conclude

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{\varphi(t+h)}\|P_{\varphi(t)})

\displaystyle=h^{\top}\varphi(t)^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\nabla\varphi(t)h+o(\|h\|^{2}).

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
16
.

The analogous proof can be found in Section 7.14 of
Polyanskiy and Wu, (2022)
. To begin, we observe that the chi-squared divergence can be written out as

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\frac{\{d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}-(\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})\}^{2}}{\lambda d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\frac{(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}-d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})^{2}}{\lambda d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}}.

We will consider the function

\phi:=f\mapsto\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\frac{f^{2}-d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}}{\{\lambda f^{2}+(1-\lambda)d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}\}^{1/2}}.

It can then be seen that
\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})=(1-\lambda)^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{h})^{2}
. When
\phi
is Lipshitz continuous and
d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{h}
is absolutely continuous with the gradient
t\mapsto\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}
, then the composition function
\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{h})
is also absolutely continuous with the gradient
t\mapsto\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{t})\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}
. In Section 7.14 of
Polyanskiy and Wu, (2022)
, it is shown that
\phi
is indeed Lipshitz continuous with the constant
(2-\lambda)/\{(1-\lambda)\lambda^{1/2}\}
. Additionally,
\phi
has a continuous derivative and we have

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}):=\begin{cases}2,&\mbox{if}\hskip 9.24994pt(x,t):d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}(x,t)>0\\
\lambda^{-1/2}&\mbox{if}\hskip 9.24994pt(x,t):d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}(x,t)=0.\end{cases}

Putting together, we have

\lim_{t\longrightarrow 0}\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{t})\dot{\gamma}_{t,u}=\dot{\gamma}_{0,u}\left(2I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}>0)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}=0)\right).

Noting that
\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{0})=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{h})^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\left(\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{h})-\phi(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{0})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\left(\int_{0}^{h}\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{s})\dot{\gamma}_{s,u}\,ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)^{2}h^{\top}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{hs_{1}})\phi^{\prime}(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n/2}_{hs_{2}})\dot{\gamma}_{hs_{1},u}\dot{\gamma}^{\top}_{hs_{2},u}\,ds_{1}\,ds_{1}\right)h.

Since
\phi^{\prime}
is continuous and
\dot{\gamma}
is the uniform integrability, asserted by
(A3)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)^{2}h^{\top}\left\{\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}^{\top}_{0}\left(4I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}>0)+\frac{1}{\lambda}I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}=0)\right)\right\}h+o(\|h\|^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)^{2}h^{\top}4\left\{\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}^{\top}_{0}+\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}^{\top}_{0}\left(\frac{1-4\lambda}{4\lambda}I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}=0)\right)\right\}h+o(\|h\|^{2})

as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
by the dominated convergence theorem. Finally, it has been established that
(A3)

\implies

(A4)
, and that the corresponding Fisher information can be computed using
4\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}^{\top}_{0}
. By the definition of the Hellinger differentiability, it follows that

\displaystyle H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})=\int\left(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}^{n/2}-d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{n/2}\right)^{2}=h^{\top}\left(\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}\right)h+o(\|h\|^{2})

as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
. Thus in view of Lemma
18
, we have

\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)

and similarly

\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\Theta}\dot{\gamma}_{0}\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}=0)=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(Q)+n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)

where
\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(0):=4\int\dot{\gamma}_{0}\,\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{\top}I(d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}=0)
is the Fisher defect. When the prior
Q
has a continuously differentiable density
q
, it must follow that
\nabla q(t)=0
whenever
q(t)=0
. This implies that the Fisher defect
\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(Q)
is zero. Thereby, we conclude that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)^{2}h^{\top}\left\{\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\left(\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))+\frac{1-4\lambda}{4\lambda}\mathcal{I}^{\dagger}(\varphi(t))\right)\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right\}h+o(\|h\|^{2}).

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
17
.

By the definition of the Hellinger differentiability, we have

\displaystyle H^{2}\left(P_{\varphi(t+h)},P_{\varphi(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\left(dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t+h)}-dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left(dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t)+\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t)}-dP^{1/2}_{\varphi(t)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left((\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t))^{\top}\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}\right)^{2}+o(\|\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t)\|^{2})

\displaystyle=\int\left((\nabla\varphi(t+\lambda h)h)^{\top}\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}\right)^{2}+o(\|\nabla\varphi(t+\lambda h)h)\|^{2})

\displaystyle=h^{\top}\nabla\varphi^{\top}(t+\lambda h)\left(\int\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}^{\top}\,d\nu\right)\nabla\varphi(t+\lambda h)h^{\top}+o(\|\nabla\varphi(t+\lambda h)h)\|^{2})

for some constant
\lambda\in[0,1]
possibley depending on
t
. Since the Fisher information is defined as
4\int\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}\dot{\xi}_{\varphi(t)}^{\top}\,d\nu
and
\nabla\varphi(t+\lambda h)\longrightarrow\nabla\varphi(t)
as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
by the continuity, we conclude that

\displaystyle H^{2}\left(P_{\varphi(t+h)},P_{\varphi(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\nabla\varphi(t)h^{\top}+o(\|h\|^{2})

as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
.

∎

Lemma 18
.

Assuming
Q
has a density function that is bounded and continuously differentiable, and
P_{t}
is Hellinger differentiable at
t=\varphi(t)
such that
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}<C
,

\displaystyle H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|^{2})

Proof of Lemma
18
.

First, the Hellinger distance between
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0}
can be written out as

\displaystyle H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})

\displaystyle=2-2\iint dP^{n/2}_{\varphi(t+h)}\,dQ^{1/2}(t+h)\,dP^{n/2}_{\varphi(t)}\,dQ^{1/2}(t)\,

\displaystyle=2-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dQ^{1/2}(t+h)dQ^{1/2}(t)\int_{\mathcal{X}}dP^{n/2}_{\varphi(t+h)}\,dP^{n/2}_{\varphi(t)}

\displaystyle=2-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dQ^{1/2}(t+h)\,dQ^{1/2}(t)\left(1-\frac{1}{2}H^{2}(P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)},P^{n}_{\varphi(t)})\right)

\displaystyle=H^{2}(Q_{h},Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,dQ^{1/2}(t+h)dQ^{1/2}(t)\,H^{2}(P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)},P^{n}_{\varphi(t)}).

When the density function
Q
is continuously differentiable, the induced location family is differentiable in quadratic mean (Example 7.8 of
Van der Vaart, (2000)
), which implies the Hellinger differentiability. By the analogous result to Lemma
17
for
\varphi(t)=t
, we have

\displaystyle H^{2}(Q_{h},Q)=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\mathcal{I}(Q)h+o(\|h\|^{2}).

Next, using the tensorization property of the Hellinger distance as well as Lemma
17
, it implies

\displaystyle H^{2}\left(P^{n}_{\varphi(t)},P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}\right)=2-2\left(1-\frac{H^{2}(P_{\varphi(t)},P_{\varphi(t+h)})}{2}\right)^{n}=2-2\left(1-h^{\top}Ch+o(\|h\|^{2})\right)^{n}

as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
where
C:=\frac{1}{8}u^{\top}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\nabla\varphi(t)u
. By treating
n\geq 1
as a fixed constant as
\|h\|\longrightarrow 0
, we have

\displaystyle 2-2\left(1-h^{\top}Ch+o(\|h\|^{2})\right)^{n}=2-2(1-nh^{\top}Ch+o(n\|h\|^{2}))=2nh^{\top}Ch+o(n\|h\|^{2}),

by a first-order Taylor expansion. Putting them together, we have

\displaystyle H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{h},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{0})=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|^{2}).

where we invoke the dominated convergence theorem to interchange the limit and integration operations, which follows by the boundedness of
\nabla\varphi
and
\mathcal{I}(\cdot)
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of asymptotic properties

This section provides the derivation of results from Section
4
. Throughout, we assume that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
is an IID observation from
P_{\theta_{0}}\in\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
and each distribution in this model is Hellinger differentiable at
\theta
.

D.1
Proofs of local asymptotic minimax theorem

In this section, we use Theorems
5
–
7
and Lemma
8
to prove the local asymptotic minimax (LAM) theorem. Let
\psi:\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable at
\theta_{0}
and
T:\mathcal{X}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be any sequence of measurable functions.
We can then state the following result:

Proposition 3
.

Assuming the setting of Theorem
1
, the following statements hold:

(i)

Theorem
5
implies the LAM theorem,

(ii)

if
(A3)
holds and the Fisher defect is zero at
\theta_{0}
, then Theorem
6
implies the LAM theorem,

(iii)

Theorem
7
implies the LAM theorem, and

(iv)

Lemma
8
implies the LAM theorem with its lower bound multiplied by the constant
C\approx 0.28953
.

Proof of Proposition
3
(i).

Since
\Phi
is arbitrary in Lemma
4
, we choose

\Phi:=\{\phi:\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}\,:\,\phi\textrm{ is continuously differentiable at $\theta_{0}$}\}.

Since
\psi\in\Phi
by the differentiability of
\psi
, it simply follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|T(X)-\psi(P_{\theta})|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\phi\in\Phi,\,Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\,\left[\Gamma_{Q,\phi}^{1/2}-\left(\int\|\psi(\theta)-\phi(\theta)\|^{2}dQ(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\right]_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\psi(t)\,dQ\right)^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+\int_{\Theta_{0}}\mathcal{I}(t)\,dQ\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{\Theta_{0}}\nabla\psi(t)\,dQ\right).

Section 4 of
Gassiat and Stoltz, (2024)
shows that the van Trees inequality indeed recovers the optimal asymptotic constant of the LAM theorem. This concludes the claim.
∎

We prove the statement (iii) first.

Proof of Proposition
3
(iii).

In this application, the parameter space considered is given by
\Theta:=B(\theta_{0},cn^{-1/2})
, which is an open
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-ball centered at
\theta_{0}
with radius
cn^{-1/2}
. A diffeomorphism
t\mapsto\varphi(t)=\theta_{0}+cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)
is defined where
\varphi_{0}
is itself a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
B([0],1)
. It follows that
\nabla\varphi(t)=cn^{-1/2}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)
. We further assume that
\|\nabla\varphi_{0}\|_{\infty}<C
by some universal constant
C
, which is defined later. Theorem
7
is then applied to the composite function
t\mapsto\widetilde{\psi}(t):=(\psi\circ\varphi)(t)
, and the following inequality is obtained:

\displaystyle\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)|^{2}

(27)

\displaystyle\geq\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h))\,dQ(t)|}{2H(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h})}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}_{+}

for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using the mean value theorem and the chain rule, it follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h)

\displaystyle=cn^{-1/2}\nabla\psi(\lambda_{1}\varphi(t)+(1-\lambda_{1})\varphi(t-h))^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t-\lambda_{2}h)^{\top}h

where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in[0,1]
are constants that can possibly depend on
t
. Next, under the Hellinger differentiability and
n
IID observations from
P_{\theta_{0}}
, it is shown by Lemma
18
that as
\|h\|_{2}\longrightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|_{2}^{2}).

The results obtained thus far are now used to evaluate the expression (
27
). Since the inequality holds for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it also holds as
\|h\|_{2}\longrightarrow 0
. We now denote
h=u\varepsilon
where
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
. This yields the following lower bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h))\,dQ(t)|}{2H(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{h})}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}_{+}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\limsup_{\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0}\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-u\varepsilon))\,dQ(t)|}{2H(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{u\varepsilon})}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-u\varepsilon)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}_{+}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\frac{c^{2}n^{-1}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\psi(\varphi(t))^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}u\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{u^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)u}.

By multiplying both sides by
n
, we have for any
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)|^{2}\geq\frac{c^{2}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}u\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{u^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)u}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)|^{2}\geq\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}u\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{u^{\top}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)u}

where the dominated convergence theorem is invoked during the last steps to exchange the limiting and the integration operations. This follows since
\varphi(t)\longrightarrow\theta_{0}
as
n\longrightarrow\infty
and both
\nabla\psi
and
\mathcal{I}
are continuous at
\theta_{0}
by assumption. Using
u:=\|M^{-1/2}\|^{-1}M^{-1/2}
where
M:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)
, it implies that

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}\cdot\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)^{-1}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right\}\cdot\nabla\psi(\theta_{0}).

Since the choice of
\varphi_{0}
was also arbitrary, we may consider

\varphi_{0}(t)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan(\|t\|/\gamma)\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)=\frac{2}{\pi\gamma}\frac{1}{1+(\|t\|/\gamma)^{2}}\frac{t}{\|t\|}.

This choice also satisfies that
\|\nabla\varphi_{0}\|_{\infty}<2(\pi\gamma)^{-1}
. Plugging them into the expression, the leading constant
2(\pi\gamma)^{-1}
will be canceled between the numerator and the denominator. Taking
\gamma\longrightarrow\infty
, the gradient of
\varphi_{0}
converges to a constant. Since the gradient of
\varphi_{0}
is uniformly bounded by construction, it is concluded that

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)|^{2}\geq\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})^{-1}\cdot\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})

in view of the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3
(ii).

Similar to the proof of Proposition
3
(iii), the parameter space is given by
\Theta:=B(\theta_{0},cn^{-1/2})
, and we define a diffeomorphism
t\mapsto\varphi(t)=\theta_{0}+cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)
where
\varphi_{0}
is itself a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
B([0],1)
. We further assume that
\|\nabla\varphi_{0}\|_{\infty}<C
by some universal constant
C
.

Theorem
6
is then applied to the composite function
t\mapsto\widetilde{\psi}(t):=(\psi\circ\varphi)(t)
, and for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following inequality is obtained:

\displaystyle\inf_{T}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{\varphi(s)}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(t)|^{2}

(28)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{h,L\geq 0}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h))\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})}-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right]_{+}.

We further assume that the prior distribution
Q
has a continuously differentiable Lebesgue density. Then under the
n
IID observations from the Hellinger differentiable distribution with the Fisher defect being zero, Lemma
16
implies as
\|h\|_{2}\longrightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\chi^{2}(Q_{h}\|Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi^{2}(P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}\|\lambda P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}+(1-\lambda)P^{n}_{\varphi(t)})\,\frac{dQ_{h}^{2}}{dQ}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+n(1-\lambda)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=h^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}(1-\lambda)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)h+o(\|h\|_{2}^{2}).

The results obtained previously are now used to evaluate the expression (
28
). Since the inequality holds for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it also holds as
\|h\|_{2}\longrightarrow 0
. Here,
h
is denoted by
u\varepsilon
where
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
. This yields the following inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h))\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})}-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-h)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\limsup_{\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0}\,\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\bigg[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-u\varepsilon))\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{u\varepsilon}\|\lambda\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{u\varepsilon}+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt-\frac{\lambda(1+1/L)}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widetilde{\psi}(t)-\widetilde{\psi}(t-u\varepsilon)|^{2}\,dQ(t)\bigg]_{+}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1+L\lambda}\left\{\frac{c^{2}n^{-1}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\psi(\varphi(t))^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}u\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{u^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}(1-\lambda)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)u}\right\}.

Since the above display holds for any
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we let
\lambda\longrightarrow 0
, which results in

\displaystyle\inf_{T}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{\varphi(s)}}|T(X)-\widetilde{\psi}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\left\{\frac{c^{2}n^{-1}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\psi(\varphi(t))^{\top}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}u\,dQ(t)|^{2}}{u^{\top}\left(\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\varphi(t))\,\nabla\varphi_{0}(t)\,dQ(t)\right)u}\right\}.

The remaining proof is identical to the proof of the second statement of Proposition
3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3
(iv).

First, we observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{\|\theta_{0}-\theta\|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=n\sup_{\|h\|<c}\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2}}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq n\sup_{\|h\|<c}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta_{0})\big|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2}}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2})\big|^{2}\right).

We now apply Lemma
8
to two points in parameter space
\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2}
, which implies

\displaystyle\sup_{\|h\|<c}\,n\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2}}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq n\left[\frac{1-H^{2}(P^{n}_{\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2}},P^{n}_{\theta_{0}})}{4}\right]_{+}|\psi(\theta_{0}+hn^{-1/2})-\psi(\theta_{0})|^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 9.24994pt\|h\|<c.

For the remaining of the proof,
h
is denoted by
u\varepsilon
where
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
0<\varepsilon<c
. We treat
\varepsilon
and
u
as fixed constants as
n\longrightarrow\infty
. First by the mean value theorem, we have

\displaystyle|\psi(\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2})-\psi(\theta_{0})|=\varepsilon n^{-1/2}\nabla\psi(\theta^{*})^{\top}u

where
\theta^{*}:=\lambda\theta_{0}+(1-\lambda)(\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2})=\theta_{0}-\lambda u\varepsilon n^{-1/2}
for
\lambda\in[0,1]
. This implies that

\displaystyle n|\psi(\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2})-\psi(\theta_{0})|^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\left(\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}u\right)^{2}+o(1)

as
n\longrightarrow\infty
, which follows by the continuity of
\nabla\psi^{\top}
at
\theta_{0}
. Next, by the Hellinger differentiability of
P_{\theta}
at
\theta_{0}
, Lemma
17
implies that the Hellinger distance between
P_{\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2}}
and
P_{\theta_{0}}
associated with
one
observation converges to

\displaystyle H^{2}(P_{\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2}},P_{\theta_{0}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u}{4n}+o(n^{-1})

as
n\longrightarrow\infty
. By the tensorization property of the Hellinger distance and the fact that
(1-Z_{n}/n)^{n}\longrightarrow\exp(-Z)
as
Z_{n}\longrightarrow Z
for
n\longrightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle H^{2}(P^{n}_{\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2}},P^{n}_{\theta_{0}})=2-2\left(1-\frac{H^{2}(P_{\theta_{0}+u\varepsilon n^{-1/2}},P_{\theta_{0}})}{2}\right)^{n}\longrightarrow 2-2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u}{8}\right)

as
n\longrightarrow\infty
. Putting them together, we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{\|\theta_{0}-\theta\|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\left[-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u}{8}\right)\right]_{+}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}u\right)^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 9.24994pt0\leq\varepsilon<c\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{and}\hskip 9.24994ptu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

It now remains to optimize the above display for
0\leq\varepsilon<c
and
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
as
c\longrightarrow\infty
. Since
u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u
is a scalar, we parameterize
\varepsilon
such that
\overline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\big(u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u\big)^{1/2}
and so we can optimize over
\overline{\varepsilon}
instead. This gives us that

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{\|\theta_{0}-\theta\|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\sup_{0\leq\overline{\varepsilon}<\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}}{8}\right)\right)_{+}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}\left(\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}u\right)^{2}\big(u^{\top}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})u\big)^{-1}

For the leading constant, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\overline{\varepsilon}<\infty}\left\{-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}}{8}\right)\right\}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}=C\approx 0.28953

and the optimal
u
is given by
u^{*}:=\|\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})^{-1/2}\|^{-1}\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})^{-1/2}
. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{\|\theta_{0}-\theta\|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big|T(X)-\psi(\theta)\big|^{2}\geq C(\nabla\psi(\theta_{0})^{\top}\,\mathcal{I}(\theta_{0})^{-1}\nabla\psi(\theta_{0}))

where
C\approx 0.28953
.
∎

D.2
Proof of Proposition
1

We first define the parametric path to be used for the proof of Proposition
1
. Following Example 25.16 of
Van der Vaart, (2000)
, we define bounded univariate parametric paths as follows:

dP_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{C_{t}}\kappa(tg(x))\,dP_{0}(x)

where
C_{t}:=\int\kappa(tg(x))\,dP_{0}
. We assume that
\kappa(0)=\kappa^{\prime}(0)=1
and
\|\kappa^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq K
and
\|\kappa^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq K
for some constant
K
. We then use the following result from
Duchi and Ruan, (2021)
.

Lemma 19

(Lemma 1 of
Duchi and Ruan, (2021)
)
.

Assuming
g\in\mathcal{T}_{P_{0}}
and
dP_{t}
is the parametric path defined as above, then as
t\longrightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{t,g}\|P_{0})=t^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}+o(t^{2})

Proof of Proposition
1
(i).

By an analogous argument from Proposition
3
, we can choose

\Phi=\{\phi:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\,:\,\phi\textrm{ is pathwise differentiable relative to }\mathcal{T}_{P_{0}}\}

for the application of Theorem
5
. We then apply the van Trees inequality along each parametric path. Although
Gassiat and Stoltz, (2024)
does not provide an explicit statement for nonparametric settings, the proof remains analogous.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1
(ii).

For each fixed
g\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
, consider a parametric path defined by
P_{t,g}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that the unknown data-generating distribution corresponds to
P_{0,g}
. We then consider the diffeomorphism
t\mapsto\varphi(t)=cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)
where
\varphi_{0}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto(-1,1)
. As the score function
g
is fixed throughout the proof, we denote
the parametric path by
P_{t}
and omit the dependency on
g
.

We apply Corollary
6.1
to the univariate functional
t\mapsto\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})
over the joint probability measures defined as

d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(x,t):=dP^{n}_{\varphi(t)}(x)\,dQ(t)\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{and}\hskip 9.24994ptd\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(x,t):=dP^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}(x)\,dQ(t+h).

we then obtain that

\displaystyle\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}~\geq~\sup_{h\in\mathbb{R}}\,\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}\|\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0})}.

By the pathwise differentiablity of the functional, it follows that

\displaystyle\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\psi(P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)})-\psi(P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t-h)})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=cn^{-1/2}(\varphi_{0}(t)-\varphi_{0}(t-h))\int\dot{\psi}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+o(cn^{-1/2}(\varphi_{0}(t)-\varphi_{0}(t-h)))

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=cn^{-1/2}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t-\lambda h)h\int\dot{\psi}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}+o(cn^{-1/2}h)

for some constant
\lambda\in[0,1]
possibly depending on
t
. After multiplying by
n
both sides and taking
n\longrightarrow\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,n\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)\,dQ(t)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,c^{2}h^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t-\lambda h)\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t-h)}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\right)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}+o(c^{2}h^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq c^{2}h^{2}\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t-\lambda h)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}+o(c^{2}h^{2})

where we use Fatou’s lemma in the last step. Specifically, the tangent space under consideration corresponds to the entire
L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
and thus
g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t-h)}\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
. Since
L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
is also a complete space, it follows that

g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\longrightarrow g_{0}\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{0}).

Hence, we can deduce that

\int\dot{\psi}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t-h)}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\longrightarrow\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g_{0}\,dP_{0}

as
n\longrightarrow\infty.

Next, we analyze the local behavior of the chi-squared divergence on the path. By Lemma
19
provided above, we obtain

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t+h)}\|P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)})

\displaystyle=c^{2}n^{-1}\{\varphi_{0}(t+h)-\varphi_{0}(t)\}^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle=c^{2}n^{-1}h^{2}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\}^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}

for some constant
\lambda\in[0,1]
. By the tensorization property of the chi-squared divergence, we have

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P^{n}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t+h)}\|P^{n}_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)})

\displaystyle=\left\{1+\chi^{2}(P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t+h)}\|P_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)})\right\}^{n}-1

\displaystyle=\left[1+c^{2}n^{-1}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\}^{2}h^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}\right]^{n}-1

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exp\left(c^{2}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\}^{2}h^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}\right)-1

as
n\longrightarrow\infty
. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\mathbb{P}_{0})

\displaystyle=\chi^{2}(Q_{h}\|Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi^{2}(P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)}\|P^{n}_{\varphi(t)})\,\frac{dQ_{h}^{2}}{dQ}

\displaystyle=\chi^{2}(Q_{h}\|Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\exp\left(c^{2}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\}^{2}h^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}\right)-1\right\}\,\frac{dQ_{h}^{2}}{dQ}

as
h\longrightarrow 0
. Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}}{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{h}\|\mathbb{P}_{0})}\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{for all }h\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\frac{c^{2}h^{2}\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t-\lambda h)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}+o(c^{2}h^{2})}{\chi^{2}(Q_{h}\|Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\exp\left(c^{2}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\}^{2}h^{2}\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}\right)-1\right\}\,\frac{dQ_{h}^{2}}{dQ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{for all }h\in\mathbb{R}.

Since the above inequality holds for any
h\in\mathbb{R}
, we take
h\longrightarrow 0
to obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}~\geq~\frac{c^{2}\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t)\,dQ\right|^{2}}{\mathcal{I}(Q)+c^{2}\left(\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t)\}^{2}\,dQ}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}~\geq~\frac{\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t)\,dQ\right|^{2}}{\left(\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t)\}^{2}\,dQ}.

As shown in the proof of Proposition
3
, it follows that
\sup_{\varphi_{0}}\,\{\mathbb{E}_{Q}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(t)\}^{2}/\mathbb{E}_{Q}\{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(t)\}^{2}=1
with
\arctan
. Finally, taking the supremum of the score functions
g
over
L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})}\,\liminf_{c\longrightarrow\infty}\,\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,n\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta,g}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}~\geq~\sup_{g\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})}\,\frac{\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}}{\int g^{2}\,dP_{0}}=\int\dot{\psi}_{0}^{2}\,dP_{0}

where the last equality follows by the fact that
L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
is linear closure and by definition, the efficient influence function
\dot{\psi}_{0}
is contained in
L_{2}^{0}(P_{0})
(See Lemma 2.2 of
Van der Vaart, (2002)
)

∎

Proof of Proposition
1
(iii).

Following the notation and the setting from the previous proof, we apply Theorem
7
to the univariate functional
t\mapsto\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})
over the joint probability measures
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(x,t)
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(x,t)
, and we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{T}\,\sup_{|\theta|<cn^{-1/2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{n}}|T(X)-\psi(\theta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left[\frac{|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)\,dQ(t)|}{2H(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{h})}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}_{+}

for all
h\in\mathbb{R}
. As shown in the previous proof, it follows that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,n\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)\,dQ(t)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\geq c^{2}h^{2}\left(\int\dot{\psi}_{0}\,g\,dP_{0}\right)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t-\lambda h)\,dQ(t)\right|^{2}+o(c^{2}h^{2})

by Fatou’s lemma. Similarly, it also follows that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,n\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\psi(P_{\varphi(t)})-\psi(P_{\varphi(t-h)})\right)^{2}\,dQ(t)\right)^{1/2}=O(c^{2}h^{2}).

Next, we study the local behavior of the Hellinger distance. Since
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
is a QMD family, the Hellinger distance associated with
one
observation follows:

\displaystyle H^{2}(P_{\varphi(t+h)},P_{\varphi(t)})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\int\left(dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t+h)}^{1/2}-dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}^{1/2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\frac{1}{4}c^{2}n^{-1}\int\{\varphi_{0}(t+h)-\varphi_{0}(t)\}^{2}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}^{2}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}+o(c^{2}n^{-1}\{\varphi_{0}(t+h)-\varphi_{0}(t)\}^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\frac{1}{4}c^{2}n^{-1}h^{2}\int\left\{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(t+\lambda h)\right\}^{2}\,g_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}^{2}\,dP_{cn^{-1/2}\varphi_{0}(t)}+o(c^{2}n^{-1}h^{2})

and by the tensorization property,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\,H^{2}\left(P^{n}_{\varphi(t+h)},P^{n}_{\varphi(t)}\right)
